Accumulated notebook:
---
**Overview**

Ali Khamenei remained Iran’s supreme leader in 2023 and therefore the state’s top authority. Formally, the office controlled national policy, the armed forces, senior military appointments, the judiciary chief, state broadcasting, and key clerical/electoral bodies. In practice, the presidency and parliament operated under that framework, while the Guardian Council, judiciary, IRGC, and the Supreme Leader’s office constrained and filtered political competition. The year was marked by continuing fallout from the 2022 “Woman, Life, Freedom” protests, intensified repression and executions, a modest diplomatic thaw with Saudi Arabia, and an economy still shaped by sanctions, inflation, and structural fragility.

## Evidence Records

### 1. Supreme leadership was the real center of power, but exercised through institutions that also constrained politics
- **Underlying fact:** Khamenei’s office remained the decisive locus of authority in 2023, but it operated through a layered system rather than single-handed day-to-day rule.
- **Why it matters:** Any assessment of 2023 policy outcomes has to attribute final responsibility to the supreme leader while recognizing that implementation ran through the presidency, judiciary, IRGC, Guardian Council, and other institutions.
- **Strongest source:** [Council on Foreign Relations, “The Islamic Republic of Iran’s Power Centers”](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers), 2026-03 (background explainer; not period-specific but structurally relevant).
- **Supporting passage:** CFR states the supreme leader is “effectively Iran’s leader for life,” that Article 110 gives the office major powers, and that the leader commands the armed forces and appoints military chiefs and IRGC/police heads. It also notes the president serves under the leader and sets policy only within those parameters. [Source lines](#) from the opened page: [turn7view0](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers)
- **Period connection:** Inherited structure, active in 2023.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Direct and strong.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The Assembly of Experts formally oversees the leader, but CFR notes this oversight is secretive and has not meaningfully checked Khamenei; that means formal checks existed but were weak in practice.
- **Cautions:** CFR is a secondary analytical source and should be read as a synthesis, not as the constitutional text itself.
- **Independent support:** [Iran Data Portal, Article 99 interpretation](https://irandataportal.syr.edu/interpretation-of-article-99); [Encyclopaedia Iranica, Guardian Council](https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/guardian-council/).

### 2. The Guardian Council and electoral supervision sharply limited political competition
- **Underlying fact:** The Guardian Council controlled electoral supervision in a way that allowed it to approve or reject candidates, reducing meaningful competition.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main institutional limit on formal electoral politics and a core reason why the regime can hold elections without permitting full political pluralism.
- **Strongest source:** [Iran Data Portal, “Interpretation of Article 99”](https://irandataportal.syr.edu/interpretation-of-article-99).
- **Supporting passage:** The portal reproduces the Guardian Council’s 1991 interpretation that Article 99 supervision is proactive and includes “confirming or rejecting the qualifications of candidates.” Opened lines: [turn5view0](https://irandataportal.syr.edu/interpretation-of-article-99)
- **Period connection:** Inherited condition, operative in 2023.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Strong but indirect; the council is not identical to Khamenei, but its structure is tightly linked to his authority because half its members and its chair are appointed through leader-linked processes.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** None that changes the basic institutional fact; the main nuance is that electoral control is shared with the Guardian Council rather than personally exercised by Khamenei in every case.
- **Cautions:** This is a constitutional interpretation record, not a 2023 event report.
- **Independent support:** [Encyclopaedia Iranica, Guardian Council](https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/guardian-council/); [CFR power centers](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers).

### 3. The Saudi-Iran détente was the main positive foreign-policy development, but it was a managed de-escalation, not a strategic opening
- **Underlying fact:** In March-April 2023 Iran and Saudi Arabia agreed to restore diplomatic relations and reopen embassies after Chinese mediation.
- **Why it matters:** This was the clearest foreign-policy success of the year and a sign that Tehran could still produce tactical regional diplomacy even amid isolation.
- **Strongest source:** [Saudi Press Agency, Joint Trilateral Statement](https://www.spa.gov.sa/en/ed7aabfefaw), 2023-03-10.
- **Supporting passage:** The statement records agreement to resume diplomatic relations, reopen embassies within two months, respect sovereignty, and revive prior security and cooperation agreements. Reuters separately reported the deal as a breakthrough between rival powers. Opened source: [turn4search0](https://www.spa.gov.sa/en/ed7aabfefaw), corroborated by [Reuters via Investing](https://www.investing.com/news/commodities-news/iran-and-saudi-arabia-agree-to-resume-ties-reopen-embassies-iranian-state-media-3027583)
- **Period connection:** Directly within 2023.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Strong but not exclusive; the deal was negotiated by security and foreign-policy officials under Khamenei’s strategic umbrella.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The détente did not remove the broader confrontation with the West, sanctions pressure, or regional proxy tensions.
- **Cautions:** The statement is diplomatic language; it shows agreement, not durable implementation or internal Iranian consensus.
- **Independent support:** Reuters report, OIC welcome statement, and later Iranian foreign-ministry confirmation.

### 4. Repression after the 2022 protests remained the dominant domestic political fact in 2023
- **Underlying fact:** The state kept detaining, intimidating, and punishing protesters, women resisting forced veiling, journalists, and relatives of dissidents; no comprehensive public accounting existed for arrests, charges, or convictions.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest lens on public safety, political freedom, media freedom, protest, and civil liberties in 2023.
- **Strongest source:** [UN OHCHR / Fact-Finding Mission oral update, 2023-07-05](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273).
- **Supporting passage:** The Mission said no official data had been made public on those arrested, detained, charged, or convicted; reports of arrests and family harassment continued; and the aftermath involved intimidation and reprisals. It also said 22,000 people had been pardoned, implying broader detention than disclosed. Opened lines: [turn7view2](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273)
- **Period connection:** Directly within 2023, though rooted in the 2022 protests.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Strong. The crackdown involved state security bodies operating under the existing political order headed by Khamenei.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The government cited pardons and amnesties, which complicate a purely one-direction repression narrative, but the same source says those releases were partial, opaque, and followed by continued arrests and reprisals.
- **Cautions:** The Mission is independent and credible, but it also reports that official transparency was absent, so some estimates rely on indirect evidence.
- **Independent support:** [U.S. State Department 2023 Iran human rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107731.html); [UN Secretary-General report record](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4025334?ln=en).

### 5. The human-rights situation worsened overall in 2023, including executions, religious repression, and coercive enforcement of dress rules
- **Underlying fact:** Iran executed 798 people in 2023, intensified restrictions on women, and increased repression against Baha’is and other groups.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest high-level quantitative sign of the regime’s security and coercive posture in the year.
- **Strongest source:** [U.S. Department of State, 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Iran](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107731.html).
- **Supporting passage:** The report says human-rights restrictions worsened, women faced enhanced enforcement of the mandatory dress code, 798 citizens were executed, and approximately 22,000 protest detainees were released under amnesty but many later rearrested. Opened lines: [turn7view1](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107731.html)
- **Period connection:** Directly about 2023.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Strong, because these are state practices under the supreme leader-centered system; however, the report attributes the actions to state agents rather than Khamenei personally issuing each order.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The February amnesty is the main mitigating fact, but it does not offset the scale of executions and repression.
- **Cautions:** The State Department report is a government source and may emphasize abuses, so it should be paired with UN and NGO material.
- **Independent support:** [UN Fact-Finding Mission](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273); [UN Secretary-General report record](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4025334?ln=en).

### 6. The economy in 2023 showed moderate growth but remained structurally constrained by sanctions, instability, and inflation
- **Underlying fact:** The World Bank described Iran’s economy as continuing to grow moderately in 2023, but with slower growth than the prior year and continuing vulnerability to sanctions and uncertainty.
- **Why it matters:** This gives the baseline for living standards, service delivery, and the regime’s economic performance.
- **Strongest source:** [World Bank, Iran Economic Monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor), especially the Spring/Summer 2023 issue listing.
- **Supporting passage:** The World Bank says Iran’s economy “continued to grow moderately for the third consecutive year albeit at a slower pace than in the previous year.” Its broader country page says the economy faced mounting pressures from structural challenges, sanctions, and shortages. Opened lines: [turn6view8](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor), [turn0view4](https://www.worldbank.org/en/ext/en/country/iran)
- **Period connection:** Directly relevant to 2023.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Moderate. These are macro outcomes shaped by policy, sanctions, and external conditions; Khamenei bears strategic responsibility but not sole causal responsibility.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The same World Bank material acknowledges some growth and employment recovery, so the picture is not one of simple collapse.
- **Cautions:** World Bank summaries are high-level; they do not by themselves explain distributional harm, provincial inequality, or the effect of repression on economic behavior.
- **Independent support:** [World Bank Macro Poverty Outlook for Iran, April 2024](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099830404052427908); [UNDP country profile](https://data.undp.org/countries-and-territories/IRN).

### 7. Later retrospective evidence confirmed the 2022-2023 protest crackdown was more lethal and widespread than the state acknowledged
- **Underlying fact:** The UN Fact-Finding Mission later concluded that the protest crackdown involved widespread unlawful killings, including up to 551 deaths, and that the state apparatus used firearms broadly.
- **Why it matters:** This is a strong retrospective measure of the scale and character of the violence that shaped 2023 political conditions.
- **Strongest source:** [UN Geneva press conference on the Fact-Finding Mission report, 2024-03-18](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/2100/hrc-press-conference-fact-finding-mission-report-on-iran-18-march-2024/6076).
- **Supporting passage:** The Mission said it found “credible figures” of up to 551 deaths, including at least 49 women and 68 children, across 26 provinces, and concluded the death of Mahsa Amini was unlawful and caused by physical violence in state custody. Opened source: [turn8search0](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/2100/hrc-press-conference-fact-finding-mission-report-on-iran-18-march-2024/6076)
- **Period connection:** Later retrospective finding about the 2022-2023 protest period.
- **Connection strength to ruler/authority:** Strong but indirect; it concerns state conduct under the Khamenei-era apparatus.
- **Complicating/contrary evidence:** The Mission notes government claims that security forces were also killed; it had limited access and no state cooperation, so the state’s side of the evidentiary record remained partly untested.
- **Cautions:** This is retrospective and based partly on interviews, forensics, and documentary reconstruction rather than full on-site access.
- **Independent support:** [UN OHCHR July 2023 update](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273); [UN report record A/HRC/55/67](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4039538).

## Information Environment and Evidence Limits

- The evidence environment was heavily distorted by censorship, intimidation, and weak official transparency.
- The UN Fact-Finding Mission said no official data had been made public on arrests, detentions, charges, or convictions tied to the protests, and that requests to the government for access went unanswered.
- State reporting on protests and repression was incomplete and contested; outside observers relied on NGO tallies, witness accounts, court documents, and forensic reconstruction.
- The government’s amnesty and pardon claims complicate one-sided readings, but they do not resolve the larger absence of transparent case-level data.
- Economic assessment was easier than protest accounting, but still limited by sanctions, uneven statistics, and the fact that macro stability can coexist with serious distributional harm.
- Media attention was uneven: repression inside Iran drew intensive rights coverage, while day-to-day governance, patronage networks, and internal elite bargaining were harder to observe.

## Questions For Deeper Research

- How much autonomous authority did Khamenei exercise over the IRGC, judiciary, and intelligence organs in specific 2023 decisions?
- Which 2023 protest, hijab-enforcement, and execution decisions can be tied to identifiable chains of command?
- How did sanctions, oil revenues, and informal patronage affect the regime’s capacity to buy compliance in 2023?
- What was the real policy meaning of the Saudi détente inside Iran’s security establishment?
- How did the Guardian Council shape the 2023-2024 electoral environment and candidate field?
- What did the state know internally about protest deaths, and how did that compare with public claims?
- Which indicators best capture living-standard deterioration for ordinary households beyond GDP growth?

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**

Khamenei remained Iran’s top authority in 2023. For nuclear and existential-risk questions, the relevant pattern is: the supreme leader set the strategic line, the Atomic Energy Organization and foreign-policy apparatus implemented it, and the IAEA/UN system kept warning that verification had been degraded and enrichment had advanced. The year’s main risk signals were continued JCPOA collapse, uranium stockpile growth, ballistic-missile/UAV allegations tied to Russia’s war in Ukraine, and Khamenei’s willingness to preserve bargaining leverage while keeping the nuclear infrastructure intact.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Khamenei’s nuclear line was conditional engagement, not disarmament**
- Source: [Reuters via RFE/RL, June 11, 2023](https://www.rferl.org/a/iran-khamenei-nuclear-deal-approval/32454257.html)
- Claim: Khamenei said a deal with the West was possible if Iran’s nuclear infrastructure remained intact, and that continued work with the IAEA under safeguards was acceptable.
- Why it matters: This is the clearest 2023 statement of his nuclear posture: negotiating room, but no willingness to trade away core nuclear capability.

2. **Iran’s verification picture remained badly degraded**
- Source: [UN Security Council note S/2023/322](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4010695)
- Claim: The Security Council transmitted an IAEA report on Iran’s verification and monitoring under resolution 2231, showing the issue remained formally active in 2023.
- Why it matters: Confirms that the nuclear file was still being tracked through UN/IAEA monitoring rather than resolved.

3. **By mid-2023 the IAEA/UN said Iran’s enriched-uranium stockpile had grown far beyond JCPOA limits**
- Source: [UN Security Council meeting SC/15344, July 6, 2023](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm)
- Claim: The UN briefing said the IAEA estimated Iran’s enriched-uranium stockpile at more than 20 times the JCPOA limit, including higher quantities enriched to 20% and 60%.
- Why it matters: This is the strongest target-year indicator that the regime increased catastrophic-risk exposure rather than reducing it.

4. **The IAEA said it could not provide full assurance about undeclared material and activities**
- Source: [IAEA Board statement / introductory safeguards statement](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/statements/introductory-statement-board-gov)
- Claim: The IAEA said it could verify non-diversion of declared material, but could not provide credible assurance that all material in Iran was peaceful because Iran was not cooperating enough.
- Why it matters: This is the core safety-and-control problem: even where declared facilities were monitored, the broader program remained insufficiently transparent.

5. **Iran publicly defended a WMD-free-zone framework while accusing others of nuclear blackmail**
- Source: [UNODA Middle East zone conference, Iran statement PDF](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/Iran_1.pdf)
- Claim: Iran told the 2023 conference that WMDs pose an “existential threat,” urged a nuclear-weapon-free Middle East, and reaffirmed compliance with its CSA commitments.
- Why it matters: This is official rhetoric relevant to 1B.2 and 1B.4, but it is largely regional-posture rhetoric and not proof of restraint.

6. **The IAEA reiterated that its role is to verify peaceful use, and the Middle East remains institutionally tied to safeguards**
- Source: [UNODA Middle East zone conference, IAEA statement PDF](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/IAEA.pdf)
- Claim: The IAEA described its continuing role in verifying peaceful use and in promoting safeguards and additional protocols in the Middle East.
- Why it matters: This gives the institutional baseline against which Iran’s opaque posture should be judged.

7. **The UN Security Council framework still treated nuclear-related transfers as tightly regulated**
- Source: [UN Security Council resolution 2231 background](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/background)
- Claim: Resolution 2231 kept nuclear-related restrictions, monitoring, and the IAEA reporting structure in force.
- Why it matters: Confirms the formal legal environment Khamenei was operating in during 2023.

8. **The procurement channel remained a formal control mechanism**
- Source: [UN Security Council procurement channel page](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/nuclear-related-transfers-and-activities-procurement-channel)
- Claim: Nuclear-related imports and transfers for Iran remained subject to advance review and approval under the JCPOA/2231 framework.
- Why it matters: Relevant to proliferation control and the availability of controlled nuclear infrastructure.

9. **The UN and member states raised allegations that Iran transferred UAVs to Russia**
- Source: [UN press release SC/15344](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm)
- Claim: The Council debate recorded allegations that Iran transferred UAVs to the Russian Federation for use in Ukraine, with Iran and Russia disputing them.
- Why it matters: Relevant to 1B.5 and 1B.6 because it concerns nuclear-capable-state behavior in broader catastrophic-risk and conflict escalation.
- Caveat: This is an allegation record, not a final adjudication.

10. **The U.S. filed supporting material on alleged Iranian UAV transfers**
- Source: [UN record S/2023/661](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4021522)
- Claim: The U.S. transmitted declassified material alleging Iranian UAV transfers to Russia in violation of resolution 2231.
- Why it matters: This is useful corroboration, though still adversarial and not neutral proof.

11. **The Secretary-General’s office said the 2231/UAV issue was still under analysis**
- Source: [UN daily briefing, June 26, 2023](https://press.un.org/en/2023/db230626.doc.htm)
- Claim: The Secretariat said it continued analyzing information on alleged UAV transfers and would report to the Council.
- Why it matters: Shows the UN had not closed the file and that the evidence was still being processed.

12. **Iran’s own UN statement linked nuclear technology with regional cooperation, not weapons**
- Source: [Iran statement in the UNODA conference PDF](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/Iran_1.pdf)
- Claim: Iran advocated peaceful nuclear cooperation and fuel-cycle collaboration in the Middle East.
- Why it matters: This is the best available pro-restraint rhetoric from an official channel, but it sits alongside continued enrichment escalation.

**Disposition by Lens**

- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk:** Partial-negative. Iran kept diplomatic space open, but the main observable policy track was advanced enrichment plus degraded verification, not risk reduction.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture:** Negative / unresolved. The year ended with stronger enrichment and weaker transparency than the inherited JCPOA posture.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Mixed. Khamenei rejected blanket closure to a deal and publicly accepted safeguards language, but no source here shows a durable restraint doctrine.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control:** Negative / weak evidence. IAEA reporting shows incomplete assurance and impaired verification.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections:** Negative / mixed. Iran kept the JCPOA/2231 machinery alive procedurally, but did not restore full compliance.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression:** Concerned / adverse. The strongest 2023 related evidence is the alleged UAV transfer to Russia.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation:** Negative / unproven. No credible evidence here shows active internal prevention; the UAV allegations cut the other way.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise:** Unclear / not well-evidenced in the inspected material. The IAEA is the independent expert body, but Iran did not empower it fully.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation:** Mixed. Khamenei accepted a deal in principle if infrastructure stayed intact, but enrichment and verification trends still pointed toward escalation risk.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks:** Weak / sparse. The only material evidence here is the UAV-to-Russia dispute and broader export-control concerns.

**Information Environment**

- The strongest limitation was **verification opacity**. The IAEA repeatedly said it could not give full assurance on undeclared material and activities.
- The nuclear file was **politically polarized**. UN records include contradictory positions from Iran, the U.S., Russia, and EU states.
- The evidence base is **asymmetric**: we have strong institutional records on stockpiles, safeguards, and UN procedures, but much weaker direct visibility into internal command decisions.
- For UAV/proliferation allegations, the evidence is **credible but contested** and should be treated as unresolved unless later corroborated by technical tracing or court-grade material.
- Official Iranian rhetoric is useful for doctrine and messaging, but it should not be treated as evidence of actual restraint without independent corroboration.

**Deeper-Research Questions**

- What exact instructions, if any, did Khamenei issue on the level of enrichment and JCPOA negotiations in 2023?
- Did Iran’s military-industrial institutions supply UAVs or missile technology to Russia, and what is the strongest technical proof?
- What internal command-and-control safeguards existed for the nuclear program in 2023?
- How much autonomy did the Atomic Energy Organization have versus the Supreme Leader’s office?
- Did any 2023 IAEA findings show new undeclared sites, material, or weaponization indicators?

**Corroborating Sources**

- [UN Security Council resolution 2231 background](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/background)
- [UN Security Council procurement channel](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/nuclear-related-transfers-and-activities-procurement-channel)
- [UN SC meeting SC/15344](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm)
- [UN daily briefing on UAV allegations](https://press.un.org/en/2023/db230626.doc.htm)
- [UN record S/2023/661](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4021522)
- [RFE/RL Reuters report on Khamenei’s June 2023 statement](https://www.rferl.org/a/iran-khamenei-nuclear-deal-approval/32454257.html)
- [UNODA conference statement by Iran](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/Iran_1.pdf)
- [UNODA conference statement by the IAEA](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/IAEA.pdf)

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**

- [UN Meetings Coverage on 2231 / allegations against Iran](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm) for more exact attribution of claims and counterclaims
- [UN Security Council notes S/2023/323, S/2023/322, and later 2023 2231 reports](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4010672?ln=en)
- [IAEA 2023 2231 reports in full](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/iaea-reports)
- [USIP Iran nuclear timeline](https://iranprimer.usip.org/resource/timeline-irans-nuclear-activities)
- [UNODA statements index for other state reactions and technical context](https://meetings.unoda.org/meeting/67899/statements)

**Inspected Candidates**
- Opened and used: UN S/2023/322, UN S/2023/323, UN SC/15344, Reuters/RFE-RL Khamenei report, UNODA conference statements page, Iran statement PDF, IAEA statement PDF, UN resolution 2231 background, UN procurement channel page, UN daily briefing June 26, UN record S/2023/661, DPPA/UN briefing on 2231.
- I did not rely on broader discovery beyond the supplied catalogue except where needed to close a concrete evidence gap.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Overview**

In 2023 Ali Khamenei’s Iran pursued a dual track on international peace and lawful security: it kept nuclear and regional coercive capabilities intact while selectively using diplomacy to reduce pressure. The year’s most important peace-related positive was the March 2023 Iran-Saudi rapprochement. The most important negative was continued nuclear escalation under weak transparency, plus unresolved allegations about Iranian weapons transfers to Russia and proxies. Formal restraint language remained present, but independent institutions continued to report incomplete cooperation, expanded enrichment, and degraded confidence in Iran’s peaceful intent.

**Evidence Records**

### 1. Iran-Saudi diplomatic normalization was the year’s clearest peace-building move
- **Fact:** Iran and Saudi Arabia agreed in March 2023 to restore diplomatic relations and reopen embassies after Chinese mediation.
- **Why it matters:** This was the strongest concrete de-escalatory step in Iran’s external relations in 2023.
- **Source:** [Joint Trilateral Statement](https://www.spa.gov.sa/en/ed7aabfefaw), Saudi Press Agency, 2023-03-10.
- **Locator:** Joint statement text announcing restoration of ties, reopening embassies, and revival of earlier agreements.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly tied to Khamenei’s strategic authority, though carried out through foreign-policy and security channels.
- **Contrary evidence:** This did not resolve broader regional conflict dynamics or the nuclear file.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters reporting and later diplomatic follow-through.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`

### 2. The JCPOA/2231 framework still governed Iran’s nuclear diplomacy
- **Fact:** In 2023 the Security Council still treated resolution 2231 and the JCPOA architecture as the operative legal framework for nuclear restrictions, monitoring, and the procurement channel.
- **Why it matters:** This is the formal law/policy baseline for lawful security in the nuclear domain.
- **Source:** [Security Council procurement channel page](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/nuclear-related-transfers-and-activities-procurement-channel), UN Security Council.
- **Locator:** Lines 11-19, 27-46, 47-69.
- **Period:** Inherited framework active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Institutional context rather than a single ruler act, but it defined Khamenei’s legal choices.
- **Contrary evidence:** The framework existed, but Iran’s compliance was disputed and incomplete.
- **Corroboration:** UN meeting coverage and IAEA statements.
- **Lens support:** `2B.7`, `2B.9`, `2B.10`

### 3. Iran publicly presented itself as supporting a WMD-free Middle East
- **Fact:** Iran’s UN statement in November 2023 framed WMDs as an existential threat, called for a WMD-free Middle East, and said it supported peaceful nuclear cooperation.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest official rhetoric of restraint and lawful regional security in the record.
- **Source:** [Iran statement to the UN WMD-free-zone conference](https://docs-library.unoda.org/Conference_on_the_Establishment_of_a_Middle_East_Zone_Free_of_Nuclear_Weapons_and_Other_Weapons_of_Mass_Destruction_-Fourth_session_%282023%29/Iran_1.pdf), 2023-11-13.
- **Locator:** PDF pages 0-2, especially lines 17-19, 33-39, 54-66.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Official regime position, not proof of operational restraint.
- **Contrary evidence:** The same year saw continued enrichment escalation and incomplete safeguards confidence.
- **Corroboration:** UN/IAEA records on the same forum and JCPOA monitoring.
- **Lens support:** `2B.3`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`

### 4. The IAEA still could not provide full assurance about the peaceful nature of Iran’s program
- **Fact:** The IAEA said it could verify declared material was not being diverted, but could not give credible assurance that all nuclear material and activities in Iran were peaceful.
- **Why it matters:** This is the central lawful-security problem: declared compliance was not enough to establish comprehensive peaceful use.
- **Source:** [IAEA introductory statement to the Board of Governors](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/statements/introductory-statement-board-governors-3).
- **Locator:** Statement section on Iran safeguards implementation.
- **Period:** Inherited condition active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system because safeguards cooperation depends on state policy.
- **Contrary evidence:** Iran claimed its program was peaceful and under safeguards.
- **Corroboration:** UN reporting and later IAEA/UN briefings.
- **Lens support:** `2B.4`, `2B.6`, `2B.9`, `2B.10`

### 5. Iran and the IAEA reached a March 2023 understanding on unresolved safeguards questions, but it did not close the file
- **Fact:** The IAEA and Iran issued a joint statement in March 2023 saying Iran would provide additional information and access on unresolved safeguards questions at three sites.
- **Why it matters:** This was the main 2023 procedural effort to reduce international nuclear risk by diplomacy and inspection.
- **Source:** [IAEA-Iran joint statement](https://www.iaea.org/fr/newscenter/pressreleases/declaration-commune-de-lorganisation-iranienne-de-lenergie-atomique-oiea-et-de-lagence-internationale-de-lenergie-atomique-aiea), 2023-03-14.
- **Locator:** Bullet points on cooperation, information, access, and verification activities.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the Iranian leadership’s decision to engage, though mediated through the Atomic Energy Organization and foreign ministry.
- **Contrary evidence:** Later 2023 UN/IAEA materials show the unresolved issues persisted.
- **Corroboration:** IAEA Board materials and UN reporting.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.9`

### 6. Iran’s enrichment and stockpile growth moved the nuclear file toward greater risk, not less
- **Fact:** By July 2023 the UN briefing said the IAEA estimated Iran’s enriched uranium stockpile was more than 20 times the JCPOA limit, including increased quantities enriched to 20% and 60%.
- **Why it matters:** This is the key outcome evidence showing the regime did not leave the international security posture safer than it inherited.
- **Source:** [UN Security Council meeting coverage SC/15344](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm), 2023-07-06.
- **Locator:** Briefing text describing stockpile growth and enrichment levels.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to state policy under Khamenei’s authority.
- **Contrary evidence:** Several states and the UN continued to call diplomacy the only viable route.
- **Corroboration:** IAEA statements and later UN briefings.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.7`, `2B.8`, `2B.10`

### 7. The IAEA said Iran was not cooperating enough for comprehensive verification
- **Fact:** The IAEA said Iran was still not providing the cooperation needed for credible assurance on undeclared nuclear material and activities.
- **Why it matters:** This reflects weak lawful security and weak acceptance of outside checks.
- **Source:** [IAEA introductory statement to the Board of Governors](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/statements/introductory-statement-board-governors-3).
- **Locator:** Iran safeguards implementation section.
- **Period:** Inherited and target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system.
- **Contrary evidence:** The March 2023 joint statement showed some willingness to engage.
- **Corroboration:** UN 2231 reporting and later IAEA briefings.
- **Lens support:** `2B.4`, `2B.6`, `2B.9`

### 8. The UN Security Council continued to treat alleged Iranian UAV transfers as a live issue
- **Fact:** In July 2023 the UN record said member states and the Secretariat had received information alleging Iranian UAV transfers to Russia, while Iran and Russia denied the claims.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main 2023 record relevant to proxy/arms-recipient control and support for a war of aggression.
- **Source:** [UN SC/15344](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15344.doc.htm), 2023-07-06.
- **Locator:** Paragraphs on Ukraine-related allegations and responses.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Potentially attributable to Iranian state policy, but not adjudicated as proven on this record alone.
- **Contrary evidence:** Iran and Russia disputed the evidence; the UN record does not itself resolve the factual dispute.
- **Corroboration:** U.S. submission to the Security Council and later UN discussions.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.5`, `2B.6`, `2B.8`, `2B.9`

### 9. The UN Secretariat said it was still analyzing the UAV allegations
- **Fact:** On 26 June 2023 the UN said it continued to analyze information about alleged Iranian UAV transfers and would report to the Security Council.
- **Why it matters:** This shows the evidentiary status was still open, which matters for careful attribution.
- **Source:** [UN daily press briefing](https://press.un.org/en/2023/db230626.doc.htm), 2023-06-26.
- **Locator:** Q&A on Iran, drones, and resolution 2231.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Institutional context, not a ruler act.
- **Contrary evidence:** None on the record; the source is about ongoing analysis.
- **Corroboration:** SC/15344 and U.S. submissions.
- **Lens support:** `2B.5`, `2B.6`, `2B.9`

### 10. The United States formally transmitted evidence on the alleged UAV transfers
- **Fact:** The U.S. sent declassified material to the Security Council alleging Iranian UAV transfers to Russia in violation of resolution 2231.
- **Why it matters:** This is an important corroborating source, though adversarial.
- **Source:** [UN Digital Library record S/2023/661](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4021522), 2023-09-12.
- **Locator:** Record summary and annex description.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Potentially attributable to Iranian state policy, not directly proven by the record summary alone.
- **Contrary evidence:** Iranian denials; the record is a member-state submission rather than an adjudication.
- **Corroboration:** UN meeting coverage and Secretariat analysis.
- **Lens support:** `2B.5`, `2B.6`, `2B.8`

### 11. The UN 2231/JCPOA framework still assumed lawful international controls on nuclear-related transfers
- **Fact:** The procurement channel required prior approval for listed nuclear-related transfers, plus notification and end-use verification.
- **Why it matters:** This is the formal lawful-security mechanism governing Iranian imports and sensitive nuclear assistance.
- **Source:** [UN Security Council procurement channel page](https://main.un.org/securitycouncil/en/content/2231/nuclear-related-transfers-and-activities-procurement-channel), UN Security Council.
- **Locator:** Lines 11-24, 47-69.
- **Period:** Inherited framework active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Institutional baseline rather than direct ruler action.
- **Contrary evidence:** The existence of controls does not show that Iran complied with them.
- **Corroboration:** UN 2231 meeting coverage.
- **Lens support:** `2B.4`, `2B.5`, `2B.7`

### 12. The year ended with no durable restoration of JCPOA compliance or full verification confidence
- **Fact:** Late-2023 UN briefing material still described the JCPOA as the best available option, while also saying Iran’s stockpile remained far above limits and IAEA verification remained incomplete.
- **Why it matters:** This is the end-of-period outcome judgment: diplomacy remained possible, but lawful peace and restraint had not been restored.
- **Source:** [UN DPPA briefing](https://dppa.un.org/en/node/95579), 2023-11-16.
- **Locator:** Briefing text on IAEA verification and stockpile status.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** State policy under Khamenei’s authority.
- **Contrary evidence:** Continued diplomatic engagement and procedural cooperation.
- **Corroboration:** IAEA statements and SC/15344.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `2B.1 Peaceful alternatives`: Mixed. There was real diplomacy with Saudi Arabia and the IAEA, but not a clear record of choosing peaceful nuclear alternatives before escalation.
- `2B.10 Lasting international peace`: Negative overall. Regional détente improved with Saudi Arabia, but nuclear and proxy-related risks remained high.
- `2B.2 Starting or prolonging war`: Negative / unresolved. The clearest adverse evidence is the alleged support for Russia’s war and continued coercive regional posture.
- `2B.3 Honest justification for force`: Mixed. Iran’s official rhetoric emphasized legality and peace, but it coexisted with disputed and escalatory conduct.
- `2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection`: Not central to this chapter’s strongest evidence; the relevant material is the lawful nuclear and transfer-control framework rather than battlefield prisoner issues.
- `2B.5 Control of proxies and arms`: Negative / unresolved. The UAV allegations and regional weapons concerns are the main evidence.
- `2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims`: Mixed. The UN process preserved scrutiny, but Iran rejected outside claims and did not resolve the allegations convincingly.
- `2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements`: Mixed. The Saudi deal was a real settlement step, but no broader durable peace settlement emerged.
- `2B.8 Responsible military resources`: Negative / unresolved. The record suggests continued military-risk capacity and external transfer concerns rather than restrained security spending.
- `2B.9 Accountability and remedy`: Weak / negative. Iran cooperated procedurally at points, but did not resolve core verification and transfer concerns.
  
**Information Environment and Limits**

- The nuclear file was unusually well documented at the institutional level, but still opaque on internal decision-making.
- Iran’s official rhetoric was easy to obtain; the harder question was whether it matched operational conduct.
- Allegations about UAV transfers to Russia were credible enough to remain in the record, but still contested and not fully adjudicated by the sources opened here.
- The strongest negative evidence comes from the IAEA and UN, which are the right sources for this chapter because they are closest to verification, legal compliance, and monitoring.
- The strongest positive evidence is the Saudi diplomatic thaw and the March IAEA-Iran cooperation statement; both are real, but neither offsets the larger risk posture by itself.

**Source Groups**

- **Accepted evidence**
  - Saudi Press Agency joint statement
  - IAEA introductory statement on Iran safeguards
  - IAEA-Iran joint statement of March 2023
  - UN SC/15344
  - UN daily briefing on UAV allegations
  - UN Digital Library record S/2023/661
  - UNODA Iran statement
  - UN Security Council procurement channel page
  - UN DPPA briefing on the JCPOA

- **Corroboration sources**
  - Reuters reporting on the Khamenei nuclear line
  - Additional IAEA and UN 2231 materials
  - U.S. and other member-state submissions on UAV allegations

- **Uninspected leads**
  - Full 2023 IAEA quarterly safeguards reports
  - Full 2023 Secretary-General reports on Iran under resolution 2231
  - USIP Iran Primer materials on drones and nuclear activities
  - Remaining Security Council documents tied to 2231
  - Any direct Khamenei speech transcript for June 2023 nuclear remarks

**Source Counts and Gaps**

- Accepted evidence records: 12
- Context records: 3
- Discovery-only records: 2
- Main gaps:
  - direct Khamenei-authored nuclear doctrine text for 2023
  - full technical proof on UAV transfers
  - finer-grained attribution for who inside the Iranian state drove each decision
  - more detail on compliance follow-through after the March IAEA-Iran statement

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

**Orientation**

Khamenei’s 2023 domestic-safety posture was not protective. The state maintained and expanded coercive control over protesters, journalists, students, women resisting forced-veiling rules, and detainees. The strongest evidence shows a continuing crackdown after the 2022 Mahsa Amini protests, broad restrictions on information and protest, large-scale arrests, and persistent impunity. There is some evidence of formal mercy gestures, but they did not produce durable restraint or effective remedy.

**Evidence Index**

### 1. The post-Amini crackdown continued through 2023
- **Fact:** Protesters, women resisting forced veiling, and relatives of dissidents continued to be arrested or harassed in 2023, while official public data on arrests, charges, and convictions remained undisclosed.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest target-year evidence that the state did not protect people from political imprisonment or intimidation.
- **Source:** [UN Fact-Finding Mission oral update](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273), 2023-07-05.
- **Locator:** Lines 55-64 and 68-71 in the opened page.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to Khamenei’s system because the crackdown was carried out by state security bodies under the supreme-leader-centered order.
- **Contrary evidence:** The government later cited pardons and releases, but those do not offset the ongoing arrests and intimidation described here.
- **Corroboration:** State Department report, Freedom House, Amnesty submission.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`

### 2. Human-rights conditions worsened in 2023, including executions and protest detention
- **Fact:** The U.S. State Department reported worsened human-rights conditions in Iran in 2023, including 798 executions, intensified dress-code enforcement, and mass protest detentions and rearrests.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest outcome-level indicator of domestic insecurity and state coercion.
- **Source:** [2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Iran](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107731.html).
- **Locator:** Executive summary lines 6-9 in the opened page.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system, though not to a single personal order.
- **Contrary evidence:** The report notes an amnesty/pardon wave, which is a mitigating but incomplete counterpoint.
- **Corroboration:** UN Fact-Finding Mission, Amnesty, Freedom House.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.6`, `3B.7`, `3B.8`, `3B.10`

### 3. The state’s violence against protesters was later found to have caused widespread deaths and serious abuse
- **Fact:** A later UN Fact-Finding Mission concluded that the protest crackdown involved credible figures of up to 551 deaths, including at least 49 women and 68 children, and that the state apparatus used firearms broadly.
- **Why it matters:** This is the strongest retrospective evidence that the 2023 domestic-safety environment was shaped by lethal state violence.
- **Source:** [UN Fact-Finding Mission press conference summary](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/2100/hrc-press-conference-fact-finding-mission-report-on-iran-18-march-2024/6076), 2024-03-18.
- **Locator:** Press conference summary and quoted mission findings.
- **Period:** Later retrospective evidence about the 2022-2023 protest period.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the state security system under Khamenei.
- **Contrary evidence:** The mission also had no state cooperation and relied on reconstruction, so exact totals remain bounded estimates.
- **Corroboration:** July 2023 mission update, Amnesty, Freedom House.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.6`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`

### 4. Iran’s information environment was heavily constrained and opaque
- **Fact:** Freedom House reported severe limits on media freedom, censorship, arrests of journalists, and heavy restrictions on internet access during and after the protests.
- **Why it matters:** This directly affects the ability of victims, journalists, and investigators to report abuse and obtain remedy.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023).
- **Locator:** Lines 30-31, 34-38, 45-49, 61-63, 92-105, 111-117.
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited context.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system, especially security forces and judiciary.
- **Contrary evidence:** None that materially weakens the core finding; the report itself documents some limited activism and reporting.
- **Corroboration:** UN mission reporting and Amnesty.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.5`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`

### 5. Amnesty described a broad impunity crisis including torture, arbitrary detention, unfair trials, and enforced disappearance
- **Fact:** Amnesty International said Iran’s ICCPR compliance problems included torture and other ill-treatment, arbitrary detention, unfair trials, unlawful force, enforced disappearance, and discrimination against women, minorities, and LGBTI people.
- **Why it matters:** This is a strong independent summary of systemic abuse and failure of remedy.
- **Source:** [Amnesty International submission to the UN Human Rights Committee](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/mde13/7091/2023/en/), 2023-09-11.
- **Locator:** Lines 61-63 of the opened page.
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited background.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system because it concerns state conduct, detention practices, and impunity.
- **Contrary evidence:** Amnesty’s submission is advocacy-oriented, so it should be paired with UN and State Department material.
- **Corroboration:** UN mission and Freedom House.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.6`, `3B.7`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`

### 6. Iran’s own UN reply did not establish independent accountability or effective remedy
- **Fact:** Iran’s reply to UN Human Rights Committee issues acknowledged the process but did not resolve the core allegations of abuse, torture, arbitrary detention, or protest repression.
- **Why it matters:** This is relevant to the question of whether complaints and oversight were genuinely independent and whether the state accepted responsibility.
- **Source:** [Replies of the Islamic Republic of Iran to the list of issues in relation to its 4th periodic report](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4018313?ln=en), 2023.
- **Locator:** UN record summary and annexed reply.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Institutional state response, not a proof of remedy.
- **Contrary evidence:** The existence of a reply shows formal engagement with the UN process, but not substantive accountability.
- **Corroboration:** Amnesty submission and UN mission reporting.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.9`

### 7. The government tightly restricted access to protest-related information
- **Fact:** Freedom House reported that authorities restricted information about the number, status, and treatment of arrested demonstrators and political prisoners after the Amini protests.
- **Why it matters:** This is directly relevant to the chapter’s evidence-environment requirement and to the feasibility of independent oversight.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023).
- **Locator:** Lines 102-105.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the state and its security organs.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material.
- **Corroboration:** UN mission, Amnesty.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`

### 8. Universities and campus spaces were targeted during the crackdown
- **Fact:** Freedom House reported that authorities raided university campuses and arrested several hundred students during the protests.
- **Why it matters:** This is concrete evidence of unsafe protest conditions and coercive response toward young people and campuses.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023).
- **Locator:** Lines 33-38 and 116-117.
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the state.
- **Contrary evidence:** The report also notes online mobilization and protest visibility, but not protection.
- **Corroboration:** UN mission and AP reporting from the same period.
- **Lens support:** `3B.2`, `3B.7`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`

### 9. The state used internet disruption as a protest-control tool
- **Fact:** Freedom House reported localized and broader internet shutdowns, blocking of WhatsApp and Instagram, and filtering of VPNs and proxies during the protest period.
- **Why it matters:** This is a key feature of a fear-based domestic safety environment and a barrier to documentation and remedy.
- **Source:** [Freedom on the Net 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-net/2023).
- **Locator:** Search result summary and report text on shutdowns and blocking.
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited pattern.
- **Attribution:** Strongly attributable to the ruler’s system.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material.
- **Corroboration:** Freedom in the World 2023 and Amnesty.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.5`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`

### 10. Formal and practical oversight remained weak
- **Fact:** Freedom House said the supreme leader controlled the key unelected institutions and that the Assembly of Experts’ oversight was secretive and ineffective in practice.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest institutional reason abuse was hard to check or remedy.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023).
- **Locator:** Lines 45-47 and 90-97.
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Directly linked to Khamenei’s position and the institutions under him.
- **Contrary evidence:** Formal oversight bodies existed, but the source says they did not meaningfully constrain the leader.
- **Corroboration:** CFR power-center analysis.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `3B.1 Protection from state abuse`: Negative. The evidence shows continued detention, torture, killings, and no effective remedy.
- `3B.10 Lasting domestic safety`: Negative. The end-of-period condition was less safe, not more safe.
- `3B.2 Control of coercive forces`: Negative. Security forces, prisons, and aligned actors were not effectively restrained or disciplined in the record opened here.
- `3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred`: Mixed-to-negative. The strongest material is operational repression rather than direct incitement language.
- `3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight`: Negative. Oversight and remedy were weak, opaque, and ineffective.
- `3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers`: Negative. Internet shutdowns and protest-control measures were used coercively.
- `3B.6 Safety without state terror`: Negative. The evidence points to fear and coercion rather than lawful protection.
- `3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups`: Negative. Women, minors, students, minorities, and protesters were exposed to targeted abuse.
- `3B.8 Safe protest and dissent`: Negative. Protest participation carried arrest, violence, and surveillance risk.
- `3B.9 Restrained crisis response`: Negative. The crisis response was militarized, censorial, and punitive rather than restrained.

**Information Environment and Limits**

- The evidence environment was highly restricted. The main limitation was not absence of events but absence of trustworthy public accounting by the state.
- UN and NGO sources were the most useful because they directly addressed arrest, killings, torture, and documentation barriers.
- Rights reporting is sensitive to access constraints and witness fear, so exact totals should be treated as credible estimates rather than perfectly complete counts.
- The state’s own public material is useful mainly for formal replies and procedural posture, not for confirming restraint.
- The strongest remaining gap is case-level attribution: who ordered what, in which chain of command, and how far responsibility extended beyond the top leadership.

**Source Groups**

- **Accepted evidence**
  - UN Fact-Finding Mission oral update, 2023-07-05
  - U.S. State Department 2023 human-rights report on Iran
  - UN Fact-Finding Mission retrospective report press conference, 2024-03-18
  - Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023
  - Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2023
  - Amnesty International submission to the UN Human Rights Committee
  - UN reply by Iran to Human Rights Committee issues
  - UN daily and mission reporting already used in the prior orientation package

- **Corroboration sources**
  - CFR power-center analysis
  - Reuters/AP reporting already surfaced in the broader workflow
  - UN Secretary-General reports already identified in the candidate pool

- **Uninspected leads**
  - Iran Human Rights 2023 annual report on executions
  - Iranian local-language civil-society reporting on protest casualties and detention
  - Full UN Secretary-General reports for 2023 and late 2023
  - AP reporting on checkpoints and university purges
  - ABC report on women’s rights activist arrests ahead of the anniversary
  - Any court-grade or investigative files on protest deaths and torture

**Source Counts and Gaps**

- Accepted evidence records: 10
- Context records: 0
- Discovery-only records: 0
- Main gaps:
  - direct chain-of-command evidence for specific abuses
  - fuller case-level casualty and detention accounting
  - more direct documentation of formal orders or written directives
  - independent remedy or accountability records beyond procedural UN replies

SOURCE_CANDIDATE_JSON: {"access_status":"opened","document_type":"UN report","publisher":"United Nations","title":"Situation of human rights in the Islamic Republic of Iran: report of the Secretary-General","url":"https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4025334?ln=en"}

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

**Orientation**

Khamenei’s 2023 political-order record was authoritarian, not competitive. The supreme leader-centered system kept elections tightly managed, neutral oversight weak, opposition activity risky, protest policing coercive, media constrained, and digital controls active. There were isolated signs of intra-system debate and a limited public demand for constitutional change, but no evidence of durable opening. The most material 2023 developments were continued protest repression after the Amini protests, ongoing candidate vetting and institutional gatekeeping, and persistent censorship and internet disruption.

**Evidence Index**

### 1. Khamenei remained the decisive authority over the political system
- **Fact:** Iran’s supreme leader remained the real center of power in 2023, with control over national policy, the armed forces, senior appointments, and key unelected institutions.
- **Why it matters:** Political freedom depends on whether elected institutions can actually contest power. In Iran they could not.
- **Source:** [Council on Foreign Relations, “The Islamic Republic of Iran’s Power Centers”](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers)
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Direct.
- **Contrary evidence:** Formal institutions existed, but not meaningful constraint.
- **Lens support:** `4B.2`, `4B.4`, `4B.5`, `4B.8`, `4B.10`

### 2. Guardian Council supervision restricted real electoral choice
- **Fact:** The Guardian Council interpreted Article 99 as proactive supervision that includes confirming or rejecting candidate qualifications.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core mechanism that prevents truly competitive elections.
- **Source:** [Iran Data Portal, “Interpretation of Article 99”](https://irandataportal.syr.edu/interpretation-of-article-99)
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Strong, though institutional rather than personal.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material to the basic point.
- **Lens support:** `4B.1`, `4B.2`, `4B.4`, `4B.8`

### 3. The post-Amini crackdown continued throughout 2023
- **Fact:** Protesters, women resisting forced veiling, and relatives of dissidents continued to be arrested or harassed, while the UN said no official disaggregated arrest/charge/conviction data had been published.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest 2023 evidence that criticism and protest were unsafe.
- **Source:** [UN Fact-Finding Mission oral update](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1688/hrc-53-fact-finding-mission-on-iran-05-july-2023/5273)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Amnesty/pardon claims did not resolve the crackdown.
- **Lens support:** `4B.3`, `4B.6`, `4B.7`, `4B.9`, `4B.10`

### 4. Human-rights conditions worsened in 2023
- **Fact:** The U.S. State Department reported worsened human-rights conditions, including 798 executions, protest detentions, and intensified dress-code enforcement.
- **Why it matters:** Political freedom is not just institutional; it is also about the cost of dissent.
- **Source:** [2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Iran](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107731.html)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some amnesties occurred, but repression remained broad.
- **Lens support:** `4B.3`, `4B.6`, `4B.7`, `4B.9`, `4B.10`

### 5. Freedom House assessed Iran as a highly repressive political system
- **Fact:** Freedom House described the supreme leader’s control over unelected institutions and the lack of meaningful political competition.
- **Why it matters:** This is a systematic cross-check on the specific 2023 evidence.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited context.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Formal elections still occurred, but not as genuine contests for power.
- **Lens support:** `4B.1`, `4B.2`, `4B.4`, `4B.5`, `4B.10`

### 6. Protest and media suppression remained central
- **Fact:** Authorities arrested journalists, restricted internet services, and suppressed coverage of the Amini protests.
- **Why it matters:** Political freedom depends on information access and the ability to publicize abuse.
- **Source:** [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material.
- **Lens support:** `4B.3`, `4B.6`, `4B.9`, `4B.10`

### 7. Internet shutdowns and platform blocking were used as political controls
- **Fact:** Freedom House reported shutdowns, platform blocking, and VPN filtering used to suppress protest coordination and obscure documentation.
- **Why it matters:** Digital freedom was actively curtailed as a method of political control.
- **Source:** [Freedom on the Net 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-net/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Online dissent still persisted despite controls.
- **Lens support:** `4B.6`, `4B.9`, `4B.3`

### 8. University spaces were not politically protected
- **Fact:** Authorities raided campuses and arrested several hundred students during the crackdown.
- **Why it matters:** This shows the regime did not protect politically active youth or university autonomy.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material.
- **Lens support:** `4B.3`, `4B.7`, `4B.9`

### 9. Amnesty identified deep failures of lawful political protection
- **Fact:** Amnesty described arbitrary detention, unfair trials, unlawful force, enforced disappearance, and discrimination against women, minorities, and LGBTI people.
- **Why it matters:** This directly addresses whether the state protected civic and political rights in practice.
- **Source:** [Amnesty International submission to the UN Human Rights Committee](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/mde13/7091/2023/en/)
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited background.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** It is an advocacy submission, so it benefits from corroboration.
- **Lens support:** `4B.3`, `4B.6`, `4B.7`, `4B.9`, `4B.10`

### 10. Oversight institutions did not meaningfully check the leader
- **Fact:** Freedom House said the Assembly of Experts’ oversight was secretive and ineffective in practice.
- **Why it matters:** Independent checks are a core measure of political freedom.
- **Source:** [Freedom in the World 2023: Iran](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Directly tied to the ruler’s system.
- **Contrary evidence:** Formal oversight bodies existed, but not as real constraints.
- **Lens support:** `4B.4`, `4B.10`

### 11. Calls for constitutional reform surfaced, but were rejected or neutralized
- **Fact:** Contemporary analysis described rare dissent and constitutional reform debates, but these did not translate into institutional change.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main “possible alternative path” evidence, and it failed to produce democratic opening.
- **Source:** [ConstitutionNet, “In Iran, supreme leader rejects calls for constitutional reform”](https://constitutionnet.org/index.php/news/iran-supreme-leader-rejects-calls-constitutional-reform)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** The existence of debate shows some pressure for change, but not acceptance by the system.
- **Lens support:** `4B.2`, `4B.8`, `4B.10`

### 12. Electoral legality existed, but only inside a closed gatekeeping system
- **Fact:** The regime preserved elections and constitutional forms while maintaining veto points that filtered candidacies and limited meaningful opposition access.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core distinction between formal electoralism and real political freedom.
- **Source:** [Iran Data Portal Article 99 interpretation](https://irandataportal.syr.edu/interpretation-of-article-99)
- **Period:** Inherited, active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material.
- **Lens support:** `4B.1`, `4B.2`, `4B.8`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `4B.1 Genuine electoral choice`: Negative. Elections existed, but the vetting regime prevented real competition.
- `4B.10 Lasting democratic resilience`: Negative. 2023 left the system at least as closed as before, with no durable democratizing reform.
- `4B.2 No entrenchment of power`: Negative. The institutional setup and repression served entrenchment.
- `4B.3 Opposition and civic freedom`: Negative. Critics, journalists, students, protesters, and activists faced repression.
- `4B.4 Independent checks on power`: Negative. Oversight bodies did not meaningfully constrain Khamenei.
- `4B.5 Politically neutral institutions`: Negative. Institutions were not politically neutral in practice.
- `4B.6 Independent information and media`: Negative. Censorship, arrests, and internet control were routine.
- `4B.7 Equal political rights`: Negative. Women, minorities, and dissenting groups faced unequal political access and protection.
- `4B.8 Term limits and transfer`: Mostly not applicable as a head-of-system lens in a hereditary-style supreme-leader order, but the broader succession/transfer structure remained closed and noncompetitive.
- `4B.9 Digital freedom and surveillance`: Negative. The state used digital controls to suppress politics.

**Information Environment**

- The evidence base is strong on repression and weak on internal decision-making.
- State transparency was poor for arrests, charges, and detention outcomes.
- The best sources here are UN rights mechanisms and systematic rights monitors.
- Official legal forms existed, but they functioned as gatekeeping devices rather than constraints.

**Source Groups**

- **Accepted evidence**
  - Iran Data Portal Article 99 interpretation
  - UN Fact-Finding Mission oral update
  - Freedom House country and internet reports
  - Amnesty submission to the Human Rights Committee
  - U.S. State Department human-rights report
  - ConstitutionNet reform analysis

- **Corroboration sources**
  - UN Secretary-General report records
  - UN Human Rights Council session material
  - Reuters/AP reporting already present in the broader workflow

- **Uninspected leads**
  - Full UN Secretary-General report text for 2023 and late 2023
  - AP report on checkpoints and university purges
  - ABC report on arrests of women’s rights activists
  - local-language protest and press-freedom reporting
  - any direct electoral administration record for 2023 candidate vetting

**Source Counts and Gaps**

- Accepted evidence records: 11
- Context records: 0
- Discovery-only records: 0
- Main gaps:
  - direct 2023 election-administration documents
  - case-level proof of specific orders from the top
  - fuller documentation of internal institutional resistance, if any
  - any meaningful remedial action after protest repression

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

**Orientation**

Iran’s 2023 economy was still under sanctions pressure, low-confidence investment conditions, and weak private-sector autonomy. The year showed moderate aggregate growth in some official and multilateral summaries, but that growth sat beside inflation, currency weakness, high poverty risk, uneven regional performance, and a business environment shaped by state-linked actors rather than open competition. Khamenei’s system did not produce a clear, durable prosperity jump in 2023; it managed macro stress without resolving the structural causes.

**Evidence Index**

### 1. Growth continued, but only modestly and with clear structural limits
- **Fact:** The World Bank said Iran’s economy continued to grow moderately in 2023, but at a slower pace than the previous year and under persistent sanctions and structural constraints.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main baseline outcome for the year.
- **Source:** [Iran Economic Monitor, World Bank](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Moderate. The outcome reflects both policy and external constraints.
- **Contrary evidence:** Modest growth did not translate into broad prosperity or durable improvement.
- **Lens support:** `5B.1`, `5B.10`, `5B.3`, `5B.9`

### 2. Household welfare remained weak despite nominal macro activity
- **Fact:** World Bank and related country-profile material continued to show Iran’s economy under pressure, with living standards constrained by inflation, sanctions, and structural fragility.
- **Why it matters:** Prosperity must be measured beyond GDP.
- **Source:** [World Bank Iran country page](https://data.worldbank.org/country/IR)
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited context.
- **Attribution:** Moderate.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some production and employment indicators were not in free fall.
- **Lens support:** `5B.10`, `5B.8`, `5B.9`

### 3. Poverty and inequality remained material concerns
- **Fact:** World Bank poverty and inequality materials show that distribution remained a major issue, with gains not evenly shared.
- **Why it matters:** This addresses the fairness of economic burdens and benefits.
- **Source:** [Poverty and Inequality Platform - Iran](https://pip.worldbank.org/country-profiles/IRN)
- **Period:** Target-year and inherited context.
- **Attribution:** Moderate.
- **Contrary evidence:** The platform is mainly descriptive and does not by itself assign blame.
- **Lens support:** `5B.8`, `5B.10`

### 4. The macroeconomy was still constrained by sanctions and policy uncertainty
- **Fact:** The World Bank’s 2023 Iran monitor framed the economy as operating under sanctions and uncertainty, with limited room for durable private investment.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main explanation for why growth did not convert into broad prosperity.
- **Source:** [World Bank Iran Economic Monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Shared between external shock and domestic policy.
- **Contrary evidence:** The state retained some ability to manage aggregate stability.
- **Lens support:** `5B.3`, `5B.4`, `5B.9`, `5B.10`

### 5. The state’s economic order remained politically concentrated
- **Fact:** Khamenei’s system still controlled key unelected institutions and major economic levers, limiting independent corrective pressure.
- **Why it matters:** Economic performance depends on whether technocrats can operate independently.
- **Source:** [Council on Foreign Relations, “The Islamic Republic of Iran’s Power Centers”](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers)
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Direct and strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Formal civilian ministries still existed, but not as independent centers of accountability.
- **Lens support:** `5B.2`, `5B.5`, `5B.7`, `5B.9`

### 6. There is evidence of weak institutional transparency and weak corrective capacity
- **Fact:** Economic governance remained in a system with weak public transparency and limited independent checks, making evidence-based correction difficult.
- **Why it matters:** A prosperity chapter needs to know whether failure can be measured and corrected.
- **Source:** [Freedom House Iran 2023 country report](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year with inherited context.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material on the core problem.
- **Lens support:** `5B.7`, `5B.5`, `5B.10`

### 7. Public welfare was not clearly prioritized over security and loyalty spending
- **Fact:** The broader state structure continued to privilege coercive and loyalty-sustaining institutions, which weakens the claim that public resources were primarily used for broad-based prosperity.
- **Why it matters:** This addresses whether the ruler pursued public prosperity versus political survival.
- **Source:** [CFR power-center analysis](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers) and linked institutional reporting.
- **Period:** Inherited structure active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Not all spending can be shown as misallocated; this is a structural inference.
- **Lens support:** `5B.1`, `5B.5`, `5B.6`, `5B.9`

### 8. The government did not show clear evidence of economic correction after failure
- **Fact:** The available sources do not show a strong 2023 pattern of admitting policy failure and changing course in a transparent, evidence-driven way.
- **Why it matters:** This is central to the “evidence and correction” lens.
- **Source:** [World Bank Iran monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor), [Freedom House country report](https://freedomhouse.org/country/iran/freedom-world/2023)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Moderate to strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some macro management continued, but not with clear public correction.
- **Lens support:** `5B.7`, `5B.9`, `5B.10`

### 9. Fair competition and predictable business rules remained weak
- **Fact:** The political economy remained one in which state-linked actors and opaque institutions heavily shaped market access and opportunity.
- **Why it matters:** This is the key obstacle to sustainable prosperity.
- **Source:** [CFR power-center analysis](https://www.cfr.org/articles/islamic-republics-power-centers)
- **Period:** Inherited and active in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Strong.
- **Contrary evidence:** Private activity still existed, but under constrained conditions.
- **Lens support:** `5B.4`, `5B.5`, `5B.10`

### 10. 2023 did not produce a clearly stronger long-run trajectory
- **Fact:** The year ended with Iran still facing sanctions, fragile investment conditions, weak redistribution, and no durable structural reform.
- **Why it matters:** This is the overall end-state judgment for the chapter.
- **Source:** [World Bank Iran monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/iran/publication/iran-economic-monitor), [World Bank country data](https://data.worldbank.org/country/IR)
- **Period:** Target-year.
- **Attribution:** Mixed, but the ruler’s system bears substantial responsibility.
- **Contrary evidence:** Aggregate growth persisted.
- **Lens support:** `5B.10`, `5B.3`, `5B.8`, `5B.9`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `5B.1 Broad and sustainable prosperity`: Weak / negative. No convincing evidence of broad prosperity-first policy.
- `5B.10 Lasting economic trajectory`: Negative. The end-of-year path was not clearly stronger or fairer.
- `5B.2 Qualified economic leadership`: Weak. The system remained politically concentrated; evidence of merit-based economic empowerment is limited.
- `5B.3 Macroeconomic stability`: Mixed. Macro growth continued, but under structural fragility and sanctions pressure.
- `5B.4 Fair economic rules`: Negative. Predictable open rules remained limited.
- `5B.5 Resistance to economic capture`: Negative. The political-economic structure favored connected actors.
- `5B.6 Productive public investment`: Unclear to weak. The sources here do not show a clear public-investment success story.
- `5B.7 Evidence and correction`: Negative. Weak transparency and limited visible correction.
- `5B.8 Fair distribution`: Negative to weak. Burdens and benefits were not shown to be shared fairly.
- `5B.9 Managing economic shocks`: Mixed. The state managed without collapse, but not in a way that resolved the underlying shocks.

**Information Environment**

- The best evidence is from World Bank country materials and broad institutional analysis.
- Iran’s official economic data is usable for baseline context but should not be treated as full proof of welfare.
- The main limitation is that many household-level, regional, and informal-economy effects are not fully visible in a single national series.
- The strongest inference is structural: the economy remained politically controlled, sanction-constrained, and weakly corrective.

**Source Groups**

- **Accepted evidence**
  - World Bank Iran Economic Monitor
  - World Bank Iran country data
  - World Bank Poverty and Inequality Platform
  - CFR power-center analysis
  - Freedom House Iran country report

- **Corroboration sources**
  - UNDP country program materials
  - World Bank Macro Poverty Outlook
  - BTI country materials already in the broader workflow

- **Uninspected leads**
  - Full World Bank Iran Economic Monitor PDF
  - IMF Article IV or staff reports if available
  - Iranian budget execution records
  - Central bank inflation and credit data
  - local investigative reporting on household conditions

**Source Counts and Gaps**

- Accepted evidence records: 10
- Context records: 0
- Discovery-only records: 0
- Main gaps:
  - direct fiscal and budget-execution detail
  - central-bank operational independence evidence
  - household income and consumption microdata
  - sector-level productivity and investment records
  - corruption/capture evidence tied to named economic institutions
